                                         Lee et al., 2022), feature learning (Yang and Hu, 2021),             without the Jacobian regulariser, we show that the training
                                         hyperparameter turning (Yang et al., 2021), and training of          can be characterised by a kernel that is defined in terms
                                         those networks (Jacot et al., 2018; Lee et al., 2019; Yang           of the derivatives of the MLP output and its Jacobian with
                                         and Littwin, 2021). Seemingly impossible questions, such             respect to the MLP parameters. We call this kernel Jacobian

                                                                11
                          An Infinite-Width Analysis on the Jacobian-Regularised Training of a Neural Network

A. Experiment Details
For the GP convergence and convergence of finite JNTK at initialisation, we utilised a synthetic dataset generated using
the Fibonacci Lattice algorithm extended to the hypersphere, which approximates the 0.5 ϵ-net of the S 3 . Regarding the
evolution of the finite JNTK during robust training, we set the values of λ to 0.01, κ to 0.1, and the learning rate to 1. These
specific values were selected to ensure that the test accuracy approached 100% by the end of 2048 epochs.
All the natural datasets first have their entries scaled to be contained in [-1, 1], then normalised to have a unit norm to match
our assumption. We include two additional datasets in the appendix, the banknote authentication dataset (Lohweg, 2013)
which is 4-dimensional data with 1372 data points, and the connectionist bench dataset (Sejnowski and Gorman, 2017)
which is 60-dimensional data with 208 data points. The banknote authentication dataset (Lohweg, 2013) contains several
data points that are nearly parallel, so the standard NTK kernel becomes nearly singular, making the comparison impossible.
To mitigate this, we filtered out some data points that are nearly parallel. In specific, for GeLU activation, we removed data
points x(i) such that |⟨x(i) , x(j) ⟩| > 0.99 for some j < i, and 0.995 for erf activation, each resulting 186, 320 data points
respectively.
For our experiments, we utilised 3 NVIDIA RTX 6000 GPUs to validate the convergence of the GP over three days.
Additionally, we employed 3 NVIDIA RTX A5000 GPUs to verify the constancy of finite JNTK during training for one day.
All other experiments were performed on CPUs, each completed in under an hour.

B. Additional Experiments
In this section, we describe additional experiments on the minimum eigenvalue assumption and also on the kernel regression
solution analysis. We repeat the same experiments in Figures 4 and 6 but with different datasets (Lohweg, 2013; Sejnowski
and Gorman, 2017).

Figure B7. The minimum eigenvalues of the standard NTK kernel and the JNTK kernel under the GeLU and erf activation functions on
the banknote authentication dataset. The x-axis is the depth of the network, and the y-axis is the minimum eigenvalue. The o-marks
indicate that the nonzero minimum eigenvalue assumption is satisfied, and the x-marks indicate that the assumption is violated. We do not
mark the points for the standard NTK kernel because the assumption is always satisfied for the standard NTK kernel.


Figures B7 and B8 show the minimum eigenvalues of the standard NTK kernel and the JNTK kernel under the GeLU and
erf activation functions on the banknote authentication dataset and the connectionist bench dataset, respectively. Both plots
show a similar tendency as Figure 6 in the main paper. Note that in the connectionist bench dataset, the assumption is
satisfied for lower-depth networks, being invalidated only for shallow GeLU networks. This can be explained by the fact
that the connectionist bench dataset has higher dimensions than the banknote authentication and forest fire datasets.
We give the kernel regression analysis results for varying datasets and activations in Figures B9, B10, B11, B12, and B13.
We use the smallest depth network that satisfies the minimum eigenvalue assumption for each dataset and activation, which
can be found in Figures B7 and B8, and also in Figure 6 in the main text.

D. Review of the Tensor-Program Framework
We quickly review the tensor-program framework by Greg Yang (Yang, 2019; 2020). To simplify the presentation, our
review describes only a simplified version of the framework where all the hidden layers have the same width n. For the
full version where the hidden layers have different widths (but these widths are sent to infinity with a fixed ratio), see the
original papers of the framework (Yang, 2019; 2020).
Tensor programs are a particular type of straight-line programs that represent computations involving neural networks, such
as forward computation and backpropagation. Each tensor program consists of three parts, namely, initialisation, main body,
and output, each of which we will explain next.
The initialisation part declares Rd -valued input variables x1 , . . . , xp , each being initialised with i.i.d. Gaussian entries, and
also d × d matrices M1 , . . . , Mq where each matrix is initialised with i.i.d. Gaussian entries and independent with all the
other matrices and the input variables. That is, for i ∈ [p], i′ ∈ [q], and k, k ′ ∈ [d] − 1,
                                                                                                 i.i.d.
                                  xi ∈ R d ,                                            (xi )k ∼ N (0, σi2 ),
                                                                                                         τ 2′
                                                                                                             
                                                                                               i.i.d.
                                Mi′ ∈ Rd×d ,                                         (Mi′ )k,k′ ∼ N 0, i .
                                                                                                          d
The input variables can be correlated. This correlation is described by a covariance matrix (Ci,i′ )i,i′ ∈[p] that is assumed
given: for i, i′ ∈ [p] and k, k ′ ∈ [d] − 1,
                                                                     (
                                                            ′ ′       Ci,i′ , if k = k ′ ;
                                             Cov((xi )k , (xi )k ) =
                                                                      0,      otherwise.

                                                                          16
                     An Infinite-Width Analysis on the Jacobian-Regularised Training of a Neural Network

The main body of a tensor program is a sequence of two types of assignments such that no input variables are assigned and
the other variables are assigned once. The two types of assignments are as follows:

MatMul h = M x or h = M ⊺ x where M is one of the matrices declared in the initialisation part, and x is a variable
   assigned by the next NonLin-type assignment before the current MatMul-type assignment.

NonLin x = ϕ(h1 , . . . , hm ) where h1 , . . . , hm are input variables or those assigned by the first MatMul-type assignment
   before the current assignment x = ϕ(h1 , . . . , hm ), the ϕ : Rm → R is a function, and this function is applied to the
   vectors h1 , . . . , hm pointwise in this assignment.

The last output part of a tensor program has the form:

                                     d−1                                                                
                                  1X                                        1                  1
                                     ψ((h1 )k , . . . , (hm )k ) or        √ v ⊺ h1 , . . . , √ v ⊺ hm
                                  d                                          d                  d
                                     k=0

where ψ is a function of type Rm → R, h1 , . . . , hm are variables, and v is an input variable.
Every variable v (which can be x or h) in a tensor program has an associated real-valued random variable Z v , which is
defined inductively. For input variables x1 , . . . , xp , we have real-valued random variables Z x1 , . . . , Z xp ∈ R that are jointly
distributed by the zero-mean multivariate Gaussian with the following covariance:

                                                      Cov(Z xi , Z xi′ ) = Ci,i′ .

For assigned variables, their random variables are defined as follows.

MatMul For the assignment h = M x or h = M ⊺ x, the corresponding Z h is the zero-mean Gaussian random variable that
                          ′
   is independent with Z h previously-defined by induction whenever the matrix M ′ in h′ = M ′ x′ (including M ⊺ ) is not
                                             ′′
   the same as M , and is correlated with Z h with h′′ = M x′′ for the same M as follows:
                                                                  ′′                     ′′
                                                    Cov(Z h , Z h ) = σM
                                                                       2    x x
                                                                         E[Z Z ]
            2
     where σM is the variance of the entries of the matrix M .

NonLin For the assignment x = ϕ(h1 , . . . , hm ), the corresponding random variable Z x is defined by the following
   equation:
                                                 Z x = ϕ(Z h1 , . . . , Z hm ).

     Note that h1 , . . . , hm are assigned before the assignment for x so that the random variables Z h1 , . . . , Z hm are defined
     by the inductive construction.

The main reason for using the tensor-program framework is the master theorem which states that as n goes to infinity, the
k-th components of variables in a tensor program jointly converge the random variables that we have just defined inductively.
Theorem D.1 (Master Theorem). If a tensor program uses only polynomially-bounded nonlinear functions ϕ in NonLin (i.e.,
there are some C, c1 , c2 > 0 such that for any u ∈ Rm , ϕ(u) ≤ C∥u∥c1 + c2 ) and it satisfies the so-called BP-likeness (see
(Yang, 2019; 2020) for the definition), then for all polymonially-bounded ψ, we have the following almost-sure convergence:

                                        d−1
                                     1X                             a.s.
                                        ψ((h1 )k , . . . , (hm )k ) → E[ψ(Z h1 , . . . , Z hm )],
                                     d
                                        k=0

as d tends to infinity.

Using this Master theorem and Proposition G.4 from (Yang, 2019), we can show that the output distribution converges to a
Gaussian random variable.

                                                                  17
                     An Infinite-Width Analysis on the Jacobian-Regularised Training of a Neural Network

Corollary D.2 (GP Convergence). Assume that a tensor program uses only polynomially-bounded nonlinear functions ϕ in
NonLin and it satisfies the so-called BP-likeness. Also, assume that the output of the tensor program is
                                                                             
                                                   1                  1
                                                  √ v ⊺ h1 , . . . , √ v ⊺ hm
                                                    d                  d
for the input random vector v such that its entries are initialised with N (0, σv2 ), the vector is not used anywhere else in the
program, and it is independent of all other input variables in the program. Then, as n tends to infinity, the output of the
program converges in distribution to the following Gaussian distribution:
                                                                    
                                             1 ⊺             1 ⊺        dist.
                                           √ v h1 , . . . , √ v hk → N (0, K)
                                              d               d
where
                                                      Kij = σv2 E[Z hi Z hj ].

E. Proof of Theorem 3.1
We express the computation of the network’s outputs and Jacobians on all the training inputs as a tensor program and use the
Master theorem D.2 to show that these outputs and Jacobians jointly converge to Gaussian random variables.
We first define the input vectors and matrices. We have the following (1 + d0 )N -many input vectors, which correspond to
the values of the preactivation on the training inputs and the Jacobians of these preactivation:
                                         (1)         (1)         (1)              (1)

For the input matrices, we have V (l) for each layer l = 2, . . . , L. The entries of V (l) are initialised with i.i.d. samples from
N (0, 1/d).
Note that we use V instead of W as opposed to the main text. This is to match the syntax of the Tensor program while
keeping the computation equivalent to the main text. In specific, the computation of matrix multiplication

So far, we implemented the forward computation of our network as a tensor program. To apply the Master theorem, we need
to check that (1) the nonlinear functions we applied are polynomially bounded and (2) the program satisfies the BP-likeness
property. The polynomial boundedness is immediate since the function we use is either ϕ or ϕ̇(a) · b, and the assumption
that ϕ is Lipschitz implies that both ϕ and ϕ̇(a) · b are polynomially bounded. For the BP-likeness, the simple GIA check
(Condition 1 of (Yang, 2020)) applies to our program, since V (L+1) is not used in any other part of the program, except
when we compute the output.
This proves Theorem 3.1.
We use this theorem to show that if we take κ small enough and d large enough, both outputs and Jacobians at initialisation
are close to zero.
Remark E.1. By the definition of ΣL and the assumption on the activiation function ϕ, for all x ∈ Rd0 with ∥x∥ = 1, the
diagonal entries of κ2 Σ(L) (x, x) are at most κ2 (M12L + 1). Thus, using the tail bound for the standard normal distribution
and also union bound, we can show that for all ϵ > 0 and x ∈ Rd0 with ∥x∥ = 1, if g ∼ GP(0, κ2 Σ(L) ), then

get, by union bound, that

F. Proof of Theorem 3.2
We start with one general result that allows us to move the derivative from the outside of an expectation to the inside when
the expectation is taken over a GP.
Theorem F.1. Let K : Rd0 × Rd0 → R be a symmetric kernel. Consider functions ψ : R → R and φ : R2 → R such that

   • ψ and φ are polynomially bounded; and

                                                                                                                                       

G. Remark on Assumption 4.4
In this section, we illustrate the difficulty of finding a sufficient condition for Assumption 4.4 by proving that one of the
standard conditions for the positive definiteness of the NTK kernel does not work for the JNTK kernel. We provide a
counterexample for the condition. To simplify the presentation, we do not impose the assumptions in Assumption 2.1. The
result for the normalised case can be obtained by scaling every matrix by 31 .
We first review two results that guarantee the positive definiteness of the NTK kernel.
Theorem G.1 (Proposition F.1 of (Du et al., 2019b)). Suppose activation is analytic and non-polynomial. Then, the standard
NTK kernel
                                                   Θ(x(1:N ) , x(1:N ) )00
has positive eigenvalues as long as no two data satisfy x(i) = cx(j) for some c ∈ R.
Theorem G.2 (Adapted from Theorem 3.2 of (Nguyen et al., 2021)). Suppose the Hermite expansion of activation
                                                            ∞
                                                 1 X
                                         σ(x) = √        µr (σ)Hr (x) exp(−x2 /2)
                                                  2π r=0

has infinitely many nonzero µr (σ). Then, the standard NTK kernel

                                                      Θ(x(1:N ) , x(1:N ) )00

has positive eigenvalues as long as no two data satisfy x(i) = cx(j) for some c ∈ R.

Proof. We use the following inequality stated in the proof of Theorem 3.2 of (Nguyen et al., 2021):

                                                            mini∈[N ] ∥x(i) ∥2r − (N − 1) maxi̸=j |⟨x(i) , x(j) ⟩|r
                λmin (Θ(x(1:N ) , x(1:N ) )00 ) ≥ µr (σ)2                                                           .
                                                                           maxi∈[N ] ∥x(i) ∥2(r−1)

Then, we can choose r large enough such that µr (σ) ̸= 0 and maxi̸=j |⟨x(i) , x(j) ⟩|r < N 1−1 , which guarantees the positive
definiteness of the NTK kernel.

Our counterexample is related to Theorem G.2. Consider neural networks with the activation function σ(x) = x2 , which is
a polynomial function. This activation satisfies the condition of Theorem G.2, which requires that the Hermite coefficients
of the activation be non-zero for infinitely many r. It is because otherwise, the summation would include exp(−x2 /2) term,
which is not a polynomial.
Theorem G.3. For any input dimension d0 and square activation σ(x) = x2 , there exists dataset {x(i) }N
                                                                                                      i=1 such that
                                                                  
                                         λmin Θ(x(1:N ) , x(1:N ) ) = 0

but                                                                       
                                               λmin Θ(x(1:N ) , x(1:N ) )00 > 0

for shallow neural networks, i.e., L = 1.

which shows that taking λγ = 4 proves the identity.
Remark G.6. We note that the bound of Lemma G.4 is not tight. If we computationally test this rank condition, the rank of
Σ(x(1:N ) , x(1:N ) ) follows
                                                        −1
                                                      N
                                                                           (
                                                                             N (2d0 −N +1)
                                   (1:N )   (1:N )
                                                      X
                                                                                    2      if d0 > N
                          rank Σ(x        ,x       )=      max(d0 − i, 0) = d0 (d0 +1)
                                                      i=0                        2         if d0 ≤ N

for non-parallel inputs x(1:N ) .
Conversely, the bound of Lemma G.5 is tight, i.e.,
                                                    rank Σ̇(x(1:N ) , x(1:N ) ) = d0
for non-parallel inputs x(1:N ) .

So it is enough to show that all the inner products here converge to constants. Then, we have the convergence of the finite
JNTK at initialisation. The tensor program in Appendix E already computes both components of the first inner product of
each summand. Applying the Master theorem to these inner products normalised by Cl−1 gives the almost-sure convergence
of these normalised inner products to Σ(l−1) (x, x′ )00 , Σ(l−1) (x, x′ )α0 , Σ(l−1) (x, x′ )0β , Σ(l−1) (x, x′ )αβ . Now it remains to
show the convergence of the second inner product of each summand. We do this by expressing the backpropagated gradients
in a tensor program.
We first include a new random vector for the initialisation, V (L+1) ∈ Rd , which is independent of all other vectors, and its
entries are i.i.d. N (0, κ2 ). The body of this program first includes the variables that appeared in Section E, and additionally
the following variables:

To apply the Master theorem, we need to check that (1) the nonlinear functions we applied are polynomially bounded and
(2) the program satisfies the BP-likeness property. We have three non-linear functions to check for polynomial boundedness:
ϕ, ϕ̇(a) · b, and ϕ̈(a) · b · c + ϕ̇(a) + d. All of these functions are polynomially bounded, since they are linear combinations
of products of ϕ, ϕ̇, ϕ̈ to the linear functions, where ϕ, ϕ̇, ϕ̈ is Lipschitz by assumption. For the BP-likeness, the simple GIA
check (Condition 1 of (Yang, 2020)) again applies to our program, since V (L+1) is not used in any other part of the program,
except when we compute the output.
The outputs of the program whose convergence is guaranteed by Theorem D.1 are followings: for i, j ∈ [N ], α, β ∈ [d0 ]

Our final computation is evaluating these nine expectations, which can be done recursively. Before proceeding further, let’s

focus on two variables, Dα Jα gi and Dgi . Their initialisation Dα Jα hi and Dhi are the same, and the recursive
definition also matches, so they are equal always. This implies that
                                 (l)            (l)                                  (l)           (l)
                              Dgi = Dα Jα gi ,                                   Dgj = Dβ Jβ gj ,
                                 (l)            (l)                                  (l)           (l)
                             Dhi = Dα Jα hi ,                                    Dhj = Dβ Jβ hj .
This implies that most of the expectations are equal, thereby we only need to compute three expectations, (17), (18), and
(22). All other expectations can be reduced to these three expectations, by equivalence we just shown or symmetry.
Before giving the proof of all equations, we first handle simple cases. Equation (17) is a term that also appears in the
standard NTK, and can be computed with the following recurrence relation,

we can show the convergence of every inner product in the layer-wise description of the finite JNTK, which proves the
desired convergence of the finite JNTK.
To utilise this theorem, we need a version that gives a convergence rate. Since the Tensor Program framework does not give
an explicit convergence rate, we will use an implicit function to give a convergence rate.
Remark H.1. There exists function F2 (·, ·, ·, ·), so that if the width of network d is large enough,
                                                                           

then the finite JNTK and the limiting JNTK satisfy

for all i, j ∈ [N ], with probability at least 1 − δ.

We often omit function argument x when it is clear from the context.
Before stating the required lemmas and proofs, we first give the brief proof idea of Theoerm 9. By the layer-wise
decomposition of the finite JNTK in (16), it is enough to show that each layer-wise part of the finite JNTK is close to its
initialisation. To show this, we can show it by showing that the inner products appearing in each summand are close to their
initialisation after perturbation, and at initialisation, the inner products are bounded with high probability. Again, to show
that the inner products after perturbation stay constant, we need to show at initialisation the norm of vectors are bounded
with high probability, and their difference after perturbation is small. Overall, we need to show the following informal
lemmas, the activations and their norms are bounded with high probability:
                                                                                       (l−1)

I.1. Bounds at initialisation
In this section, we will prove bounds holding at initialisation, especially the lemmas formalising Equation 32 and 33. All the
lemmas here are stated for single data x, but we can apply the Union-bound to data indices, showing that the results hold for
all x(i) with probability at least 1 − N · δ. Similarly for those applied to the Jacobian values, we can apply the Union-bound
to the input dimension indices, showing equivalent results with probability at least 1 − N d0 δ.
Lemma I.2. Fix the fail probability δ > 0, and the network depth L. If the network width satisfies both d ≥ Ω(log(1/δ))
and d ≥ exp(Ω(L)), then
                                         (l)

                                                                             √
                                       g0 (x) ≤ 2 d,           h0 (x) ≤ 2 d,

holds for all l ∈ [L] with probability at least 1 − δ.

Lemma I.3. Fix the fail probability δ > 0, and the network depth L. If the network width satisfies d ≥ Ω(log(L/δ)), then
                                        (l)              √                 (l)           √

Lemma I.6. Suppose some random variable X with X > 0 almost surely. For any M > 0, there exists some random
variable Z ≤ 1 that satisfies
                                                d
                                          Z · X = X|X ≤ M.

Proof. We prove this via stochastic dominance.
We can show that X (first order) stochastically dominates X|X ≤ M , by showing that for any non-decreasing u,

                          E[u(X)] = P (X > M ) E[u(X)|X > M ] + P (X ≤ M ) E[u(X)|X ≤ M ]
                                  ≥ P (X > M )u(M ) + P (X ≤ M ) E[u(X)|X ≤ M ]
                                      ≥ P (X > M ) E[u(X)|X ≤ M ] + P (X ≤ M ) E[u(X)|X ≤ M ]
                                      = E[u(X)|X ≤ M ].
Now from the property of stochastic dominance, this implies that there exists some random variable W ≥ 0 such that
                                                              X = (X|X ≤ M ) + W
which then after rearranging, we obtain
                                                       X −W                 d
                                                              ·X
                                                              X|X ≤ M =
                                                         X
with Z = (X − W )/X ≤ 1 which is guaranteed by X > 0 and W ≥ 0.

                    An Infinite-Width Analysis on the Jacobian-Regularised Training of a Neural Network

I.2. Bounds after perturbation
We now prove
        √     that the vectors that appeared in previous sections are stable after perturbation of the weight matrices. We
consider dω perturbation over the network weights, which models the perturbation due to the gradient. We additionally
            √                                    (l)     (l)            (l)      (l)
assume the ω d log d perturbation over the ∥WA − W0 ∥∞ and ∥WA − W0 ∥1 , which are the maximum of column
sum and row sums, respectively.
We will later let the perturbation ω be small enough to prove the global convergence, so throughout this section, we will
assume that ω ≤ 1 to write ω p ≤ ω.
Since it is hard to interpret the order of quantifiers in the lemmas, we will mention here explicitly, which applies to all
lemmas in Section I.2. We first specify the fail probability and network depth. Then there exists a bound on ω and d, which
                                                  (l)
implies that the results hold for all choices of WA satisfying the condition on ω, for all l ∈ [L] and α ∈ [d0 ] simultaneously,
with probability at least 1 − δ. This result holds for any choice of x, so to extend this proof to all training data x(1:N ) , we
can apply the Union-bound to results weakened fail probability guarantee 1 − N δ.

                                    |Θd,θA (x, x′ )ij − Θd,θ0 (x, x′ )ij | ≤ κ2 ω exp(O(L)) log d

for any i, j ∈ {0} ∪ [d0 ]. We finally compute the bound on Frobenius norm as follows:

                                   ∥Θd,θA (x, x′ ) − Θd,θ0 (x, x′ )∥F ≤ κ2 ωd0 exp(O(L)) log d.




J. Proof of Theorem 4.5
We now prove that the finite JNTK stays constant during the gradient flow. We consider the weight to evolve along the
gradient flow of the objective L,
                                                          ∂L(θt )
                                                  θ̇t = −
                                                             θt
where we write each layer’s weights as θt = {W (l) (t)}L+1
                                                       l=1 .

As stated in Assumption 4.4, we will assume that the smallest eigenvalue of the matrix Θ(x(1:N ) , x(1:N ) ) is greater than 0,
with its value λ0 . The proof of this theorem is based on two lemmas, whose condition and the results are reversed.
Lemma J.1. First, assume that the width network d is large enough to satisfy
                                                       ϵ                           
                                 d ≥ F1 x(1:N ) , L, δ,    , d ≥ F2 x(1:N ) , L, δ, ϵ
                                                        κ
where F1 is defined in Remark E.1 and the F2 is defined in Remark H.1.
Further assume that the network width d and ω satisfy

                                                  d ≥ Ω(N d0 exp(L)/δ),

                                                 ω ≤ (log d)−2L

Then with probability at least 1 − δ over the random initialisation, the following holds for all t0 . If all of the matrices do not
change a lot for all 0 ≤ t < t0 , i.e.,

then we have linear convergence for 0 ≤ t ≤ t0 , i.e.,

Proof. By the assumption on d, with Theorem 4.3, we can assume that the finite JNTK at initialisation is close to its limit,

Now we are ready to prove Theorem 4.5. We first give a detailed statement of this theorem.
Theorem J.3. Fix the fail probability δ, and error ϵ. Suppose that Assumption 4.4 holds with the smallest eigenvalue λ0 .
Suppose that the width d, the coefficient κ, and the threshold ω satisfy

                                                             1

                                                                           
                                                              λ0 λ
                                               ω≤O                            ,

                                               ω ≤ (log d)−3L

Then with probability at least 1 − δ, for all t ≥ 0,

                                          Θd,θt (x(i) , x(j) ) − κ2 Θ(x(i) , x(j) )       ≤ κ2 ϵ.

for all i, j ∈ [N ].

Proof. From the assumption of κ, we can show that
                                                                               δ

where g ∼ N (0, κ2 Σ(L) ), and together with the first assumption on d, the network outputs and their Jacobian are bounded
by 1 at initialisation with probability at least 1 − δ/2.
We will first prove that the linear convergence and the weights stay close to their initialisation. This can be proven by
contradiction argument with Lemma J.2 and J.1.
Suppose that either linear convergence does not happen,

                                                                      κ2 λλ0 t
                                                                                      
                                                L(θt ) > L(θ0 ) exp −
                                                                         N

                                                                √

Write τ as the first violation time that this happens,

                             τ = argmin {t : Linear Convergence fail or weight is far from init.} .
                                    0≤t

Then by definition, the linear convergence is satisfied, and the weights are close to its initialisation for all 0 ≤ t < τ . Now
Lemma J.1 shows that at time τ , the linear convergence happens, and similarly, Lemma J.2 show that the weights at time τ
also stay close to its initialisation. This is a contradiction, which shows that our assumption, of the existence of a violation
point is false.
So now we can assume that

for all time t ≥ 0, i, j ∈ [N ].

K. Proof of Theorem 4.6
Before presenting the proof, we need several additional definitions to be used in the proof. We define the feature function of
limiting JNTK ϕ(x), which satisfies
                                                  ϕ(x)⊺ ϕ(x′ ) = Θ(x, x′ )
with ϕ0 (x), . . . , ϕd0 (t) the columns of this feature function.

L. Proofs of Theorems 4.5 and 4.6 for Gradient Descent
Consider the loss evaluation at k + 1 time step with abusing notation of unn (θk+1 ), then

                     ∥unn (θk+1 ) − y∥22
                   =∥y − unn (θk ) + unn (θk ) − unn (θk+1 )∥22
                   =∥y − unn (θk )∥22 − 2⟨y − unn (θk ), unn (θk+1 ) − unn (θk )⟩ + ∥unn (θk+1 ) − unn (θk )∥22
